Oh, goodness, no. I certainly wouldn't say that. I mean, a good night's rest is quite important, isn't it? I wouldn't want you to think I'm staying up all hours of the night doing goodness-knows-what!

I really do try my best to keep a very sensible bedtime schedule. I hope that's the right way to go about it? I'd hate for you to think I wasn't taking proper care of my health.

I hope I'm explaining it alright.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel unusually energetic, like you don't need much sleep or that your thoughts are racing faster than usual?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no, I wouldn't say that. I definitely need my sleep! At my age, a good night's rest is such a blessing, isn't it? I certainly don't stay up all hours or feel like I can just go without rest. I hope that's the kind of thing you were asking? \n\nAnd as for my thoughts... well, they don't really race. It's nothing scary or out of control like that. It's just that I always have a little to-do list going in my head, you know? Just thinking about the next cabinet to tidy or the next little project I can help with. I really do try to be productive and useful. I hope I'm not giving you the wrong impression—my mind feels quite fine, I just... I just like to keep moving. Does that help answer your question, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel worried or anxious about not being productive or about the tasks you have to do?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say worried... or anxious. Those sound so serious, don't they? I really don't want to sound like a complainer or like I can't handle things. \n\nI mean, I suppose I just like to feel useful. It's just a little urge to make sure everything is taken care of. I don't sit around wringing my hands or anything like that. I just... prefer to be up and doing things. It's perfectly normal to want a tidy house and to help out, right? I hope that doesn't sound like I'm overly anxious, Doctor. I really am quite content, just... busy. Is that the sort of thing you were wondering about?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel restless or have a hard time staying seated, even when you're not actively working on a task?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... \"restless\" sounds a bit like a misbehaving child, doesn't it? I certainly try to use my best manners. But... I suppose, if I'm being perfectly honest... when I'm at the senior center meetings, or just sitting through the long announcements at church, I do get this funny little... ants-in-my-chair feeling. \n\nI don't ever mean to be disruptive, I promise! It's just that I find I have to shift around in my seat a good bit, or maybe I'll stand up to fetch a quick cup of coffee, or just tidy and straighten the papers in front of me... just to get through the sitting part. I always try to do it quietly so I don't bother anyone else. I hope that doesn't make me sound terribly disrespectful to the people speaking. Is that the kind of thing you were asking about, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Do you ever feel physically agitated or like your body just won’t let you relax, even when you’re not trying to be productive or helpful?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, \"agitated\" is such a strong word, isn't it? It makes it sound like I'm angry or throwing a fit, and I promise you I'm not like that at all. I really do try to be pleasant and agreeable. \n\nI wouldn't say my body won't *let* me relax... it's just that I don't really care for sitting idle, you know? If I'm just sitting in my recliner watching television, well, my hands might just start looking for something to do, like folding a napkin or maybe just tapping along to a tune. But it's not a bad feeling, truly. It's just... a little extra energy, I suppose. I don't feel upset or anything like that. I hope I'm explaining this right? I really do want to be helpful and answer your questions properly, Doctor.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Have you ever noticed that you need less sleep than usual, or that you feel just as energetic even if you haven’t slept as much as you normally would?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I certainly wouldn't say that. I mean, a good night's rest is quite important, isn't it? I wouldn't want you to think I'm staying up all hours of the night doing goodness-knows-what! \n\nIf I happen to miss my sleep for some reason, I definitely feel tired the next day, just like anyone else would. I might still try to keep my hands busy during the day, just to stick to my routine and be helpful, but I certainly don't feel full of energy if I haven't rested properly. I really do try my best to keep a very sensible bedtime schedule. I hope that's the right way to go about it? I'd hate for you to think I wasn't taking proper care of my health.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, my, no. I certainly wouldn't just get up and wander about in a waiting room or a movie theater! That would be terribly rude to the other people, wouldn't it? I was always taught to be very respectful of others in public spaces. 

If I'm waiting to see my doctor—well, present company excepted, of course!—or watching a nice film, I just sit quietly. I mean, I might cross and uncross my ankles a bit, or perhaps thumb through one of those magazines they leave out, just to pass the time politely. But I certainly don't feel like I absolutely *can't* stay in my seat. I'd never want to make a fuss or draw attention to myself like that. I hope that makes sense? I always try to be on my very best behavior in those sorts of places, I promise.

It's just that I find I have to shift around in my seat a good bit, or maybe I'll stand up to fetch a quick cup of coffee, or just tidy and straighten the papers in front of me... just to get through the sitting part. If I'm just sitting in my recliner watching television, well, my hands might just start looking for something to do, like folding a napkin or maybe just tapping along to a tune. I certainly wouldn't say that. I mean, a good night's rest is quite important, isn't it? I wouldn't want you to think I'm staying up all hours of the night doing goodness-knows-what! \n\nIf I happen to miss my sleep for some reason, I definitely feel tired the next day, just like anyone else would. I might still try to keep my hands busy during the day, just to stick to my routine and be helpful, but I certainly don't feel full of energy if I haven't rested properly. I really do try my best to keep a very sensible bedtime schedule. I hope that's the right way to go about it? I'd hate for you to think I wasn't taking proper care of my health.\n\nDoctor: In situations where you’re expected to sit still, like watching a movie or waiting in a waiting room, do you find yourself needing to move or feel like you can’t stay seated even if there’s no task to do?\n\nPatient: Oh, my, no. I was always taught to be very respectful of others in public spaces. \n\nIf I'm waiting to see my doctor—well, present company excepted, of course!—or watching a nice film, I just sit quietly. I mean, I might cross and uncross my ankles a bit, or perhaps thumb through one of those magazines they leave out, just to pass the time politely. Oh, well... no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, if there's truly nothing to be done, I think I'm quite capable of just enjoying a quiet afternoon. I'm not a restless person, really, I promise.

It's just that I find I have to shift around in my seat a good bit, or maybe I'll stand up to fetch a quick cup of coffee, or just tidy and straighten the papers in front of me... just to get through the sitting part. I always try to do it quietly so I don't bother anyone else. I hope that doesn't make me sound terribly disrespectful to the people speaking. Is that the kind of thing you were asking about, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Do you ever feel physically agitated or like your body just won’t let you relax, even when you’re not trying to be productive or helpful?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, \"agitated\" is such a strong word, isn't it? It makes it sound like I'm angry or throwing a fit, and I promise you I'm not like that at all. I really do try to be pleasant and agreeable. \n\nI wouldn't say my body won't *let* me relax... it's just that I don't really care for sitting idle, you know? If I'm just sitting in my recliner watching television, well, my hands might just start looking for something to do, like folding a napkin or maybe just tapping along to a tune. But it's not a bad feeling, truly. It's just... a little extra energy, I suppose. I don't feel upset or anything like that. I hope I'm explaining this right? I really do want to be helpful and answer your questions properly, Doctor.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Have you ever noticed that you need less sleep than usual, or that you feel just as energetic even if you haven’t slept as much as you normally would?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I certainly wouldn't say that. I mean, a good night's rest is quite important, isn't it? I wouldn't want you to think I'm staying up all hours of the night doing goodness-knows-what! \n\nIf I happen to miss my sleep for some reason, I definitely feel tired the next day, just like anyone else would. I might still try to keep my hands busy during the day, just to stick to my routine and be helpful, but I certainly don't feel full of energy if I haven't rested properly. I really do try my best to keep a very sensible bedtime schedule. I hope that's the right way to go about it? I'd hate for you to think I wasn't taking proper care of my health.\n\nDoctor: In situations where you’re expected to sit still, like watching a movie or waiting in a waiting room, do you find yourself needing to move or feel like you can’t stay seated even if there’s no task to do?\n\nPatient: Oh, my, no. I certainly wouldn't just get up and wander about in a waiting room or a movie theater! That would be terribly rude to the other people, wouldn't it? I was always taught to be very respectful of others in public spaces. \n\nIf I'm waiting to see my doctor—well, present company excepted, of course!—or watching a nice film, I just sit quietly. I mean, I might cross and uncross my ankles a bit, or perhaps thumb through one of those magazines they leave out, just to pass the time politely. But I certainly don't feel like I absolutely *can't* stay in my seat. I'd never want to make a fuss or draw attention to myself like that. I hope that makes sense? I always try to be on my very best behavior in those sorts of places, I promise.\n\nDoctor: When you're completely alone at home and there's absolutely nothing that needs to be done, do you still feel like you can't quite relax or settle down?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, if there's truly nothing to be done, I think I'm quite capable of just enjoying a quiet afternoon. I'm not a restless person, really, I promise. \n\nI might... well, I might find a little something to do, like straightening the doilies or maybe reorganizing my knitting basket, but that's just because I like keeping a tidy home, you know? It's not that I *can't* settle down. I just prefer to keep my hands busy rather than just staring at the walls. It feels nice to accomplish little things, even if it's just sorting out my buttons. Oh, an inner tension? Well... I suppose... maybe just a tiny bit, sometimes? I certainly don't want to sound like a bundle of nerves, because I'm really quite cheerful most of the time! 

I do sometimes get this... well, I call it an "ants-in-my-chair" feeling. It happens more when I'm sitting through the long announcements at church, or during the weekly meetings at the senior center. I just get a little fidgety, I suppose. I might shift around in my seat a bit, or perhaps excuse myself to get a quick cup of coffee, or just quietly straighten the papers on the table in front of me. Just little things to help me get through the sitting part, you know? 

It's not that I'm terribly anxious or anything like that, I promise! I just feel a bit better if I can move just a smidge. That's not too unusual for someone my age, is it? I hope you don't think I'm being disruptive during church. I really try to be as quiet as a mouse about it.

I hope I'm explaining it alright.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel unusually energetic, like you don't need much sleep or that your thoughts are racing faster than usual?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no, I wouldn't say that. I definitely need my sleep! At my age, a good night's rest is such a blessing, isn't it? I certainly don't stay up all hours or feel like I can just go without rest. I hope that's the kind of thing you were asking? \n\nAnd as for my thoughts... well, they don't really race. It's nothing scary or out of control like that. It's just that I always have a little to-do list going in my head, you know? Just thinking about the next cabinet to tidy or the next little project I can help with. I really do try to be productive and useful. I hope I'm not giving you the wrong impression—my mind feels quite fine, I just... I just like to keep moving. Does that help answer your question, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel worried or anxious about not being productive or about the tasks you have to do?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say worried... or anxious. Those sound so serious, don't they? I really don't want to sound like a complainer or like I can't handle things. \n\nI mean, I suppose I just like to feel useful. It's just a little urge to make sure everything is taken care of. I don't sit around wringing my hands or anything like that. I just... prefer to be up and doing things. It's perfectly normal to want a tidy house and to help out, right? I hope that doesn't sound like I'm overly anxious, Doctor. I really am quite content, just... busy. Is that the sort of thing you were wondering about?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel restless or have a hard time staying seated, even when you're not actively working on a task?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... \"restless\" sounds a bit like a misbehaving child, doesn't it? I certainly try to use my best manners. But... I suppose, if I'm being perfectly honest... when I'm at the senior center meetings, or just sitting through the long announcements at church, I do get this funny little... ants-in-my-chair feeling. \n\nI don't ever mean to be disruptive, I promise! It's just that I find I have to shift around in my seat a good bit, or maybe I'll stand up to fetch a quick cup of coffee, or just tidy and straighten the papers in front of me... just to get through the sitting part. I always try to do it quietly so I don't bother anyone else. I hope that doesn't make me sound terribly disrespectful to the people speaking. Is that the kind of thing you were asking about, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Do you ever feel physically agitated or like your body just won’t let you relax, even when you’re not trying to be productive or helpful?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, \"agitated\" is such a strong word, isn't it? It makes it sound like I'm angry or throwing a fit, and I promise you I'm not like that at all. I really do try to be pleasant and agreeable. \n\nI wouldn't say my body won't *let* me relax... it's just that I don't really care for sitting idle, you know? If I'm just sitting in my recliner watching television, well, my hands might just start looking for something to do, like folding a napkin or maybe just tapping along to a tune. But it's not a bad feeling, truly. It's just... a little extra energy, I suppose. I don't feel upset or anything like that. I hope I'm explaining this right? I really do want to be helpful and answer your questions properly, Doctor.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Have you ever noticed that you need less sleep than usual, or that you feel just as energetic even if you haven’t slept as much as you normally would?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I certainly wouldn't say that. I mean, a good night's rest is quite important, isn't it? I wouldn't want you to think I'm staying up all hours of the night doing goodness-knows-what! \n\nIf I happen to miss my sleep for some reason, I definitely feel tired the next day, just like anyone else would. I might still try to keep my hands busy during the day, just to stick to my routine and be helpful, but I certainly don't feel full of energy if I haven't rested properly. I really do try my best to keep a very sensible bedtime schedule. I hope that's the right way to go about it? I'd hate for you to think I wasn't taking proper care of my health.\n\nDoctor: In situations where you’re expected to sit still, like watching a movie or waiting in a waiting room, do you find yourself needing to move or feel like you can’t stay seated even if there’s no task to do?\n\nPatient: Oh, my, no. I certainly wouldn't just get up and wander about in a waiting room or a movie theater! That would be terribly rude to the other people, wouldn't it? I was always taught to be very respectful of others in public spaces. \n\nIf I'm waiting to see my doctor—well, present company excepted, of course!—or watching a nice film, I just sit quietly. I mean, I might cross and uncross my ankles a bit, or perhaps thumb through one of those magazines they leave out, just to pass the time politely. But I certainly don't feel like I absolutely *can't* stay in my seat. I'd never want to make a fuss or draw attention to myself like that. I hope that makes sense? I always try to be on my very best behavior in those sorts of places, I promise.\n\nDoctor: When you're completely alone at home and there's absolutely nothing that needs to be done, do you still feel like you can't quite relax or settle down?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, if there's truly nothing to be done, I think I'm quite capable of just enjoying a quiet afternoon. I'm not a restless person, really, I promise. \n\nI might... well, I might find a little something to do, like straightening the doilies or maybe reorganizing my knitting basket, but that's just because I like keeping a tidy home, you know? It's not that I *can't* settle down. I just prefer to keep my hands busy rather than just staring at the walls. It feels nice to accomplish little things, even if it's just sorting out my buttons. That's perfectly normal, isn't it? I certainly wouldn't want you to think I'm pacing the halls or anything silly like that. I really do enjoy my peace and quiet.\n\nDoctor: Even when there’s no task to do and you're alone, do you ever feel an inner sense of restlessness or tension, like you can't quite settle your mind or body, even if you're not actively trying to be productive?\n\nPatient: Oh, an inner tension? Well... I suppose... maybe just a tiny bit, sometimes? I certainly don't want to sound like a bundle of nerves, because I'm really quite cheerful most of the time! \n\nI do sometimes get this... well, I call it an \"ants-in-my-chair\" feeling. It happens more when I'm sitting through the long announcements at church, or during the weekly meetings at the senior center. I just get a little fidgety, I suppose. I might shift around in my seat a bit, or perhaps excuse myself to get a quick cup of coffee, or just quietly straighten the papers on the table in front of me. Just little things to help me get through the sitting part, you know? \n\nIt's not that I'm terribly anxious or anything like that, I promise! I just feel a bit better if I can move just a smidge. That's not too unusual for someone my age, is it? I hope you don't think I'm being disruptive during church. I really try to be as quiet as a mouse about it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Oh, goodness, no. I wouldn't say it's just... moving for the sake of moving. That sounds a bit erratic, doesn't it? I certainly wouldn't want you to think I'm just wiggling about for no reason at all!

Oh, no, I really don't think so. I mean... just moving my hands or feet for absolutely no reason at all? That sounds a bit... well, a bit odd, doesn't it? I certainly wouldn't want you to think I'm just sitting there twitching or anything unseemly like that.

Being helpful and active is a good thing, isn't it? It's just that... well, even at 87, I seem to have a really hard time just sitting down and being still. Does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining it alright.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel unusually energetic, like you don't need much sleep or that your thoughts are racing faster than usual?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no, I wouldn't say that. I definitely need my sleep! At my age, a good night's rest is such a blessing, isn't it? I certainly don't stay up all hours or feel like I can just go without rest. I hope that's the kind of thing you were asking? \n\nAnd as for my thoughts... well, they don't really race. It's nothing scary or out of control like that. It's just that I always have a little to-do list going in my head, you know? Just thinking about the next cabinet to tidy or the next little project I can help with. I really do try to be productive and useful. I hope I'm not giving you the wrong impression—my mind feels quite fine, I just... I just like to keep moving. Does that help answer your question, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel worried or anxious about not being productive or about the tasks you have to do?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say worried... or anxious. Those sound so serious, don't they? I really don't want to sound like a complainer or like I can't handle things. \n\nI mean, I suppose I just like to feel useful. It's just a little urge to make sure everything is taken care of. I don't sit around wringing my hands or anything like that. I just... prefer to be up and doing things. It's perfectly normal to want a tidy house and to help out, right? I hope that doesn't sound like I'm overly anxious, Doctor. I really am quite content, just... busy. Is that the sort of thing you were wondering about?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel restless or have a hard time staying seated, even when you're not actively working on a task?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... \"restless\" sounds a bit like a misbehaving child, doesn't it? I certainly try to use my best manners. But... I suppose, if I'm being perfectly honest... when I'm at the senior center meetings, or just sitting through the long announcements at church, I do get this funny little... ants-in-my-chair feeling. \n\nI don't ever mean to be disruptive, I promise! It's just that I find I have to shift around in my seat a good bit, or maybe I'll stand up to fetch a quick cup of coffee, or just tidy and straighten the papers in front of me... just to get through the sitting part. I always try to do it quietly so I don't bother anyone else. I hope that doesn't make me sound terribly disrespectful to the people speaking. Is that the kind of thing you were asking about, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Do you ever feel physically agitated or like your body just won’t let you relax, even when you’re not trying to be productive or helpful?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, \"agitated\" is such a strong word, isn't it? It makes it sound like I'm angry or throwing a fit, and I promise you I'm not like that at all. I really do try to be pleasant and agreeable. \n\nI wouldn't say my body won't *let* me relax... it's just that I don't really care for sitting idle, you know? If I'm just sitting in my recliner watching television, well, my hands might just start looking for something to do, like folding a napkin or maybe just tapping along to a tune. But it's not a bad feeling, truly. It's just... a little extra energy, I suppose. I don't feel upset or anything like that. I hope I'm explaining this right? I really do want to be helpful and answer your questions properly, Doctor.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Have you ever noticed that you need less sleep than usual, or that you feel just as energetic even if you haven’t slept as much as you normally would?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I certainly wouldn't say that. I mean, a good night's rest is quite important, isn't it? I wouldn't want you to think I'm staying up all hours of the night doing goodness-knows-what! \n\nIf I happen to miss my sleep for some reason, I definitely feel tired the next day, just like anyone else would. I might still try to keep my hands busy during the day, just to stick to my routine and be helpful, but I certainly don't feel full of energy if I haven't rested properly. I really do try my best to keep a very sensible bedtime schedule. I hope that's the right way to go about it? I'd hate for you to think I wasn't taking proper care of my health.\n\nDoctor: In situations where you’re expected to sit still, like watching a movie or waiting in a waiting room, do you find yourself needing to move or feel like you can’t stay seated even if there’s no task to do?\n\nPatient: Oh, my, no. I certainly wouldn't just get up and wander about in a waiting room or a movie theater! That would be terribly rude to the other people, wouldn't it? I was always taught to be very respectful of others in public spaces. \n\nIf I'm waiting to see my doctor—well, present company excepted, of course!—or watching a nice film, I just sit quietly. I mean, I might cross and uncross my ankles a bit, or perhaps thumb through one of those magazines they leave out, just to pass the time politely. But I certainly don't feel like I absolutely *can't* stay in my seat. I'd never want to make a fuss or draw attention to myself like that. I hope that makes sense? I always try to be on my very best behavior in those sorts of places, I promise.\n\nDoctor: When you're completely alone at home and there's absolutely nothing that needs to be done, do you still feel like you can't quite relax or settle down?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, if there's truly nothing to be done, I think I'm quite capable of just enjoying a quiet afternoon. I'm not a restless person, really, I promise. \n\nI might... well, I might find a little something to do, like straightening the doilies or maybe reorganizing my knitting basket, but that's just because I like keeping a tidy home, you know? It's not that I *can't* settle down. I just prefer to keep my hands busy rather than just staring at the walls. It feels nice to accomplish little things, even if it's just sorting out my buttons. That's perfectly normal, isn't it? I certainly wouldn't want you to think I'm pacing the halls or anything silly like that. I really do enjoy my peace and quiet.\n\nDoctor: Even when there’s no task to do and you're alone, do you ever feel an inner sense of restlessness or tension, like you can't quite settle your mind or body, even if you're not actively trying to be productive?\n\nPatient: Oh, an inner tension? Well... I suppose... maybe just a tiny bit, sometimes? I certainly don't want to sound like a bundle of nerves, because I'm really quite cheerful most of the time! \n\nI do sometimes get this... well, I call it an \"ants-in-my-chair\" feeling. It happens more when I'm sitting through the long announcements at church, or during the weekly meetings at the senior center. I just get a little fidgety, I suppose. I might shift around in my seat a bit, or perhaps excuse myself to get a quick cup of coffee, or just quietly straighten the papers on the table in front of me. Just little things to help me get through the sitting part, you know? \n\nIt's not that I'm terribly anxious or anything like that, I promise! I just feel a bit better if I can move just a smidge. That's not too unusual for someone my age, is it? I hope you don't think I'm being disruptive during church. I really try to be as quiet as a mouse about it.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: When you feel that inner restlessness or 'ants-in-my-chair' feeling, are you aware of needing to move just for the sake of moving, even when there’s no task to do or no goal in mind?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I wouldn't say it's just... moving for the sake of moving. That sounds a bit erratic, doesn't it? I certainly wouldn't want you to think I'm just wiggling about for no reason at all! \n\nWhenever I do shift around or get up, there's always a little purpose to it, I think. Even if it's just to... well, to smooth a wrinkle in my skirt or fetch a peppermint from my purse. It's never just moving with absolutely nothing in mind. I always try to be quite deliberate, you know? \n\nI hope I'm explaining that properly... I just wouldn't want to give the impression that I'm out of control of my own body or doing things without a reason. That would be dreadful. Does that make sense, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: When you feel that urge to move or fidget, are there times when you're not trying to accomplish anything specific, like adjusting your clothes or organizing something, but you just feel the need to move your hands or feet without a clear reason?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I really don't think so. I mean... just moving my hands or feet for absolutely no reason at all? That sounds a bit... well, a bit odd, doesn't it? I certainly wouldn't want you to think I'm just sitting there twitching or anything unseemly like that. \n\nIf I'm moving my foot, it's usually because it's fallen asleep, or maybe I'm just tapping along to a nice hymn in my head. And my hands... well, like I said, I like to keep them busy with my knitting or tidying. I don't think I ever just move them without knowing why. That would be quite strange, wouldn't it? I hope you don't think I'm doing things like that without realizing it. I'm usually very aware of what I'm doing. Is that the sort of answer you were looking for?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Even when there’s no task to do and you're alone, do you ever feel an inner sense of restlessness or tension, like you can't quite settle your mind or body, even if you're not actively trying to be productive?\n\nPatient: Oh, an inner tension? Well... I suppose... maybe just a tiny bit, sometimes? I certainly don't want to sound like a bundle of nerves, because I'm really quite cheerful most of the time! \n\nI do sometimes get this... well, I call it an \"ants-in-my-chair\" feeling. It happens more when I'm sitting through the long announcements at church, or during the weekly meetings at the senior center. I just get a little fidgety, I suppose. I might shift around in my seat a bit, or perhaps excuse myself to get a quick cup of coffee, or just quietly straighten the papers on the table in front of me. Just little things to help me get through the sitting part, you know? \n\nIt's not that I'm terribly anxious or anything like that, I promise! I just feel a bit better if I can move just a smidge. That's not too unusual for someone my age, is it? I hope you don't think I'm being disruptive during church. I really try to be as quiet as a mouse about it.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: When you feel that inner restlessness or 'ants-in-my-chair' feeling, are you aware of needing to move just for the sake of moving, even when there’s no task to do or no goal in mind?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I wouldn't say it's just... moving for the sake of moving. That sounds a bit erratic, doesn't it? I certainly wouldn't want you to think I'm just wiggling about for no reason at all! \n\nWhenever I do shift around or get up, there's always a little purpose to it, I think. Even if it's just to... well, to smooth a wrinkle in my skirt or fetch a peppermint from my purse. It's never just moving with absolutely nothing in mind. I always try to be quite deliberate, you know? \n\nI hope I'm explaining that properly... I just wouldn't want to give the impression that I'm out of control of my own body or doing things without a reason. That would be dreadful. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, just a general discomfort... well, I suppose... maybe? I mean, sometimes sitting in those wooden pews for a long time can make anyone's back a little stiff, don't you think? 

But just a... a vague discomfort that makes me move for no reason? Oh, I don't think I'd call it that. That makes it sound like I'm restless in a bothersome way, and I'm really not! I'm quite content most of the time. If I shift, it's really just to get a little more comfortable, like anyone would. I'm sure you must adjust your posture in that chair of yours from time to time, right? 

I just... I want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture. I wouldn't want you to write down that I'm uncomfortable in my own skin or anything silly like that. I'm just a normal older lady trying to stay comfortable. That's perfectly alright, isn't it?

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, goodness. I... I really don't think my body ever feels like it *has* to be doing something just to feel right. That sounds so... so demanding, doesn't it? Like I'm at the mercy of my own nerves! 

I mean, I suppose sometimes I just prefer to be busy. It's nice to feel useful, even if it's just a small thing like folding a napkin or smoothing the tablecloth. But it's not because I feel wrong if I don't do it. It's just a preference, really. A habit of a lifetime, you could say. 

I certainly don't feel driven by some... some inner compulsion that I can't ignore. I wouldn't want you to think I'm sitting at home feeling all out of sorts unless my hands are moving! I'm quite peaceful, truly. I hope that's the kind of answer you need? I just want to be sure I'm giving you the right idea.

Oh, lying in bed... well... no, not really. I mean, when it's time to sleep, I just close my eyes and try to rest. It would be quite awful to be lying there tossing and turning, feeling like I *had* to move, wouldn't it? I'm sure people who have that must be so exhausted.

It's just that I always have a little to-do list going in my head, you know? Just thinking about the next cabinet to tidy or the next little project I can help with. I really do try to be productive and useful. I hope I'm not giving you the wrong impression—my mind feels quite fine, I just... I just like to keep moving. Does that help answer your question, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel worried or anxious about not being productive or about the tasks you have to do?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say worried... or anxious. Those sound so serious, don't they? I really don't want to sound like a complainer or like I can't handle things. \n\nI mean, I suppose I just like to feel useful. It's just a little urge to make sure everything is taken care of. I don't sit around wringing my hands or anything like that. I just... prefer to be up and doing things. It's perfectly normal to want a tidy house and to help out, right? I hope that doesn't sound like I'm overly anxious, Doctor. I really am quite content, just... busy. Is that the sort of thing you were wondering about?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel restless or have a hard time staying seated, even when you're not actively working on a task?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... \"restless\" sounds a bit like a misbehaving child, doesn't it? I certainly try to use my best manners. But... I suppose, if I'm being perfectly honest... when I'm at the senior center meetings, or just sitting through the long announcements at church, I do get this funny little... ants-in-my-chair feeling. \n\nI don't ever mean to be disruptive, I promise! It's just that I find I have to shift around in my seat a good bit, or maybe I'll stand up to fetch a quick cup of coffee, or just tidy and straighten the papers in front of me... just to get through the sitting part. I always try to do it quietly so I don't bother anyone else. I hope that doesn't make me sound terribly disrespectful to the people speaking. Is that the kind of thing you were asking about, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Do you ever feel physically agitated or like your body just won’t let you relax, even when you’re not trying to be productive or helpful?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, \"agitated\" is such a strong word, isn't it? It makes it sound like I'm angry or throwing a fit, and I promise you I'm not like that at all. I really do try to be pleasant and agreeable. \n\nI wouldn't say my body won't *let* me relax... it's just that I don't really care for sitting idle, you know? If I'm just sitting in my recliner watching television, well, my hands might just start looking for something to do, like folding a napkin or maybe just tapping along to a tune. But it's not a bad feeling, truly. It's just... a little extra energy, I suppose. I don't feel upset or anything like that. I hope I'm explaining this right? I really do want to be helpful and answer your questions properly, Doctor.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Have you ever noticed that you need less sleep than usual, or that you feel just as energetic even if you haven’t slept as much as you normally would?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I certainly wouldn't say that. I mean, a good night's rest is quite important, isn't it? I wouldn't want you to think I'm staying up all hours of the night doing goodness-knows-what! \n\nIf I happen to miss my sleep for some reason, I definitely feel tired the next day, just like anyone else would. I might still try to keep my hands busy during the day, just to stick to my routine and be helpful, but I certainly don't feel full of energy if I haven't rested properly. I really do try my best to keep a very sensible bedtime schedule. I hope that's the right way to go about it? I'd hate for you to think I wasn't taking proper care of my health.\n\nDoctor: In situations where you’re expected to sit still, like watching a movie or waiting in a waiting room, do you find yourself needing to move or feel like you can’t stay seated even if there’s no task to do?\n\nPatient: Oh, my, no. I certainly wouldn't just get up and wander about in a waiting room or a movie theater! That would be terribly rude to the other people, wouldn't it? I was always taught to be very respectful of others in public spaces. \n\nIf I'm waiting to see my doctor—well, present company excepted, of course!—or watching a nice film, I just sit quietly. I mean, I might cross and uncross my ankles a bit, or perhaps thumb through one of those magazines they leave out, just to pass the time politely. But I certainly don't feel like I absolutely *can't* stay in my seat. I'd never want to make a fuss or draw attention to myself like that. I hope that makes sense? I always try to be on my very best behavior in those sorts of places, I promise.\n\nDoctor: When you're completely alone at home and there's absolutely nothing that needs to be done, do you still feel like you can't quite relax or settle down?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, if there's truly nothing to be done, I think I'm quite capable of just enjoying a quiet afternoon. I'm not a restless person, really, I promise. \n\nI might... well, I might find a little something to do, like straightening the doilies or maybe reorganizing my knitting basket, but that's just because I like keeping a tidy home, you know? It's not that I *can't* settle down. I just prefer to keep my hands busy rather than just staring at the walls. It feels nice to accomplish little things, even if it's just sorting out my buttons. That's perfectly normal, isn't it? I certainly wouldn't want you to think I'm pacing the halls or anything silly like that. I really do enjoy my peace and quiet.\n\nDoctor: Even when there’s no task to do and you're alone, do you ever feel an inner sense of restlessness or tension, like you can't quite settle your mind or body, even if you're not actively trying to be productive?\n\nPatient: Oh, an inner tension? Well... I suppose... maybe just a tiny bit, sometimes? I certainly don't want to sound like a bundle of nerves, because I'm really quite cheerful most of the time! \n\nI do sometimes get this... well, I call it an \"ants-in-my-chair\" feeling. It happens more when I'm sitting through the long announcements at church, or during the weekly meetings at the senior center. I just get a little fidgety, I suppose. I might shift around in my seat a bit, or perhaps excuse myself to get a quick cup of coffee, or just quietly straighten the papers on the table in front of me. Just little things to help me get through the sitting part, you know? \n\nIt's not that I'm terribly anxious or anything like that, I promise! I just feel a bit better if I can move just a smidge. That's not too unusual for someone my age, is it? I hope you don't think I'm being disruptive during church. I really try to be as quiet as a mouse about it.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: When you feel that inner restlessness or 'ants-in-my-chair' feeling, are you aware of needing to move just for the sake of moving, even when there’s no task to do or no goal in mind?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I wouldn't say it's just... moving for the sake of moving. That sounds a bit erratic, doesn't it? I certainly wouldn't want you to think I'm just wiggling about for no reason at all! \n\nWhenever I do shift around or get up, there's always a little purpose to it, I think. Even if it's just to... well, to smooth a wrinkle in my skirt or fetch a peppermint from my purse. It's never just moving with absolutely nothing in mind. I always try to be quite deliberate, you know? \n\nI hope I'm explaining that properly... I just wouldn't want to give the impression that I'm out of control of my own body or doing things without a reason. That would be dreadful. Does that make sense, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: When you feel that urge to move or fidget, are there times when you're not trying to accomplish anything specific, like adjusting your clothes or organizing something, but you just feel the need to move your hands or feet without a clear reason?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I really don't think so. I mean... just moving my hands or feet for absolutely no reason at all? That sounds a bit... well, a bit odd, doesn't it? I certainly wouldn't want you to think I'm just sitting there twitching or anything unseemly like that. \n\nIf I'm moving my foot, it's usually because it's fallen asleep, or maybe I'm just tapping along to a nice hymn in my head. And my hands... well, like I said, I like to keep them busy with my knitting or tidying. I don't think I ever just move them without knowing why. That would be quite strange, wouldn't it? I hope you don't think I'm doing things like that without realizing it. I'm usually very aware of what I'm doing. Is that the sort of answer you were looking for?\n\nDoctor: When you feel that need to shift around or move a bit, is it ever just a discomfort in your body that makes you want to move, even if there's nothing specific you're trying to fix or organize?\n\nPatient: Oh, just a general discomfort... well, I suppose... maybe? I mean, sometimes sitting in those wooden pews for a long time can make anyone's back a little stiff, don't you think? \n\nBut just a... a vague discomfort that makes me move for no reason? Oh, I don't think I'd call it that. That makes it sound like I'm restless in a bothersome way, and I'm really not! I'm quite content most of the time. If I shift, it's really just to get a little more comfortable, like anyone would. I'm sure you must adjust your posture in that chair of yours from time to time, right? \n\nI just... I want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture. I wouldn't want you to write down that I'm uncomfortable in my own skin or anything silly like that. I'm just a normal older lady trying to stay comfortable. That's perfectly alright, isn't it?\n\nDoctor: When you feel that urge to move or shift around, even slightly, is there ever a moment where you feel like it's not about being comfortable or doing a task, but more like your body just doesn't feel right unless you're doing something, even if it's small?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness. I... I really don't think my body ever feels like it *has* to be doing something just to feel right. That sounds so... so demanding, doesn't it? Like I'm at the mercy of my own nerves! \n\nI mean, I suppose sometimes I just prefer to be busy. It's nice to feel useful, even if it's just a small thing like folding a napkin or smoothing the tablecloth. But it's not because I feel wrong if I don't do it. It's just a preference, really. A habit of a lifetime, you could say. \n\nI certainly don't feel driven by some... some inner compulsion that I can't ignore. I wouldn't want you to think I'm sitting at home feeling all out of sorts unless my hands are moving! I'm quite peaceful, truly. I hope that's the kind of answer you need? I just want to be sure I'm giving you the right idea.\n\nDoctor: When you're completely still, like when you're lying in bed trying to fall asleep, do you ever feel like your body just wants to keep moving, even just a little, even if there's no reason to?\n\nPatient: Oh, lying in bed... well... no, not really. I mean, when it's time to sleep, I just close my eyes and try to rest. It would be quite awful to be lying there tossing and turning, feeling like I *had* to move, wouldn't it? I'm sure people who have that must be so exhausted. \n\nI suppose sometimes it takes a little while to settle in, but that's just because I'm thinking about the day or... or maybe my hip aches a tiny bit, which is perfectly normal at my age, you know. But I don't feel like my body is trying to keep moving on its own. I'm usually quite still once I'm tucked in. I wouldn't want you to think I'm thrashing about in the dark! I'm a very quiet sleeper, really. Does that sound normal to you? I hope I'm being helpful.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Oh, I don't think I'd call it that. That makes it sound like I'm restless in a bothersome way, and I'm really not! I'm quite content most of the time. I... I really don't think my body ever feels like it *has* to be doing something just to feel right. That sounds so... so demanding, doesn't it? Like I'm at the mercy of my own nerves! \n\nI mean, I suppose sometimes I just prefer to be busy. It's nice to feel useful, even if it's just a small thing like folding a napkin or smoothing the tablecloth. But it's not because I feel wrong if I don't do it. It's just a preference, really. A habit of a lifetime, you could say. \n\nI certainly don't feel driven by some... some inner compulsion that I can't ignore. I wouldn't want you to think I'm sitting at home feeling all out of sorts unless my hands are moving! I'm quite peaceful, truly. I hope that's the kind of answer you need? I just want to be sure I'm giving you the right idea.\n\nDoctor: When you're completely still, like when you're lying in bed trying to fall asleep, do you ever feel like your body just wants to keep moving, even just a little, even if there's no reason to?\n\nPatient: Oh, lying in bed... well... no, not really. I mean, when it's time to sleep, I just close my eyes and try to rest. It would be quite awful to be lying there tossing and turning, feeling like I *had* to move, wouldn't it? I'm sure people who have that must be so exhausted. \n\nI suppose sometimes it takes a little while to settle in, but that's just because I'm thinking about the day or... or maybe my hip aches a tiny bit, which is perfectly normal at my age, you know. I'm a very quiet sleeper, really. Does that sound normal to you? I hope I'm being helpful.\n\n[Last Question]\nDoctor: Do you ever notice any unusual sensations in your legs, like tingling, crawling, or an urge to move them, especially when you're sitting still or lying down?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, tingling or crawling? Oh my, no. That sounds terribly unpleasant, doesn't it? Like... like having bugs on you! No, I've never felt anything strange like that in my legs. 

I mean, sometimes my legs get a little heavy if I've been on my feet too long, perhaps doing a bit too much gardening or standing at the sink. But that's just a normal tiredness, I'm sure. It's certainly not an urge to move them when I'm trying to rest. When I sit down in my armchair with a cup of tea, my legs are very happy to just stay right where they are! 

I wouldn't want you to worry that I have some sort of... nervous condition in my legs. They're perfectly fine, just a bit older now. That's a good thing, isn't it? That I don't have those crawling feelings you mentioned? I do try to take good care of myself.

It makes it sound like I'm angry or throwing a fit, and I promise you I'm not like that at all. I really do try to be pleasant and agreeable. \n\nI wouldn't say my body won't *let* me relax... it's just that I don't really care for sitting idle, you know? If I'm just sitting in my recliner watching television, well, my hands might just start looking for something to do, like folding a napkin or maybe just tapping along to a tune. But it's not a bad feeling, truly. It's just... a little extra energy, I suppose. I don't feel upset or anything like that. I hope I'm explaining this right? I really do want to be helpful and answer your questions properly, Doctor.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Have you ever noticed that you need less sleep than usual, or that you feel just as energetic even if you haven’t slept as much as you normally would?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I certainly wouldn't say that. I mean, a good night's rest is quite important, isn't it? I wouldn't want you to think I'm staying up all hours of the night doing goodness-knows-what! \n\nIf I happen to miss my sleep for some reason, I definitely feel tired the next day, just like anyone else would. I might still try to keep my hands busy during the day, just to stick to my routine and be helpful, but I certainly don't feel full of energy if I haven't rested properly. I really do try my best to keep a very sensible bedtime schedule. I hope that's the right way to go about it? I'd hate for you to think I wasn't taking proper care of my health.\n\nDoctor: In situations where you’re expected to sit still, like watching a movie or waiting in a waiting room, do you find yourself needing to move or feel like you can’t stay seated even if there’s no task to do?\n\nPatient: Oh, my, no. I certainly wouldn't just get up and wander about in a waiting room or a movie theater! That would be terribly rude to the other people, wouldn't it? I was always taught to be very respectful of others in public spaces. \n\nIf I'm waiting to see my doctor—well, present company excepted, of course!—or watching a nice film, I just sit quietly. I mean, I might cross and uncross my ankles a bit, or perhaps thumb through one of those magazines they leave out, just to pass the time politely. But I certainly don't feel like I absolutely *can't* stay in my seat. I'd never want to make a fuss or draw attention to myself like that. I hope that makes sense? I always try to be on my very best behavior in those sorts of places, I promise.\n\nDoctor: When you're completely alone at home and there's absolutely nothing that needs to be done, do you still feel like you can't quite relax or settle down?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, if there's truly nothing to be done, I think I'm quite capable of just enjoying a quiet afternoon. I'm not a restless person, really, I promise. \n\nI might... well, I might find a little something to do, like straightening the doilies or maybe reorganizing my knitting basket, but that's just because I like keeping a tidy home, you know? It's not that I *can't* settle down. I just prefer to keep my hands busy rather than just staring at the walls. It feels nice to accomplish little things, even if it's just sorting out my buttons. That's perfectly normal, isn't it? I certainly wouldn't want you to think I'm pacing the halls or anything silly like that. I really do enjoy my peace and quiet.\n\nDoctor: Even when there’s no task to do and you're alone, do you ever feel an inner sense of restlessness or tension, like you can't quite settle your mind or body, even if you're not actively trying to be productive?\n\nPatient: Oh, an inner tension? Well... I suppose... maybe just a tiny bit, sometimes? I certainly don't want to sound like a bundle of nerves, because I'm really quite cheerful most of the time! \n\nI do sometimes get this... well, I call it an \"ants-in-my-chair\" feeling. It happens more when I'm sitting through the long announcements at church, or during the weekly meetings at the senior center. I just get a little fidgety, I suppose. I might shift around in my seat a bit, or perhaps excuse myself to get a quick cup of coffee, or just quietly straighten the papers on the table in front of me. Just little things to help me get through the sitting part, you know? \n\nIt's not that I'm terribly anxious or anything like that, I promise! I just feel a bit better if I can move just a smidge. That's not too unusual for someone my age, is it? I hope you don't think I'm being disruptive during church. I really try to be as quiet as a mouse about it.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: When you feel that inner restlessness or 'ants-in-my-chair' feeling, are you aware of needing to move just for the sake of moving, even when there’s no task to do or no goal in mind?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I wouldn't say it's just... moving for the sake of moving. That sounds a bit erratic, doesn't it? I certainly wouldn't want you to think I'm just wiggling about for no reason at all! \n\nWhenever I do shift around or get up, there's always a little purpose to it, I think. Even if it's just to... well, to smooth a wrinkle in my skirt or fetch a peppermint from my purse. It's never just moving with absolutely nothing in mind. I always try to be quite deliberate, you know? \n\nI hope I'm explaining that properly... I just wouldn't want to give the impression that I'm out of control of my own body or doing things without a reason. That would be dreadful. Does that make sense, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: When you feel that urge to move or fidget, are there times when you're not trying to accomplish anything specific, like adjusting your clothes or organizing something, but you just feel the need to move your hands or feet without a clear reason?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I really don't think so. I mean... just moving my hands or feet for absolutely no reason at all? That sounds a bit... well, a bit odd, doesn't it? I certainly wouldn't want you to think I'm just sitting there twitching or anything unseemly like that. \n\nIf I'm moving my foot, it's usually because it's fallen asleep, or maybe I'm just tapping along to a nice hymn in my head. And my hands... well, like I said, I like to keep them busy with my knitting or tidying. I don't think I ever just move them without knowing why. That would be quite strange, wouldn't it? I hope you don't think I'm doing things like that without realizing it. I'm usually very aware of what I'm doing. Is that the sort of answer you were looking for?\n\nDoctor: When you feel that need to shift around or move a bit, is it ever just a discomfort in your body that makes you want to move, even if there's nothing specific you're trying to fix or organize?\n\nPatient: Oh, just a general discomfort... well, I suppose... maybe? I mean, sometimes sitting in those wooden pews for a long time can make anyone's back a little stiff, don't you think? \n\nBut just a... a vague discomfort that makes me move for no reason? Oh, I don't think I'd call it that. That makes it sound like I'm restless in a bothersome way, and I'm really not! I'm quite content most of the time. If I shift, it's really just to get a little more comfortable, like anyone would. I'm sure you must adjust your posture in that chair of yours from time to time, right? \n\nI just... I want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture. I wouldn't want you to write down that I'm uncomfortable in my own skin or anything silly like that. I'm just a normal older lady trying to stay comfortable. That's perfectly alright, isn't it?\n\nDoctor: When you feel that urge to move or shift around, even slightly, is there ever a moment where you feel like it's not about being comfortable or doing a task, but more like your body just doesn't feel right unless you're doing something, even if it's small?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness. I... I really don't think my body ever feels like it *has* to be doing something just to feel right. That sounds so... so demanding, doesn't it? I really do try to be pleasant and agreeable. \n\nI wouldn't say my body won't *let* me relax... it's just that I don't really care for sitting idle, you know? If I'm just sitting in my recliner watching television, well, my hands might just start looking for something to do, like folding a napkin or maybe just tapping along to a tune. But it's not a bad feeling, truly. It's just... a little extra energy, I suppose. I don't feel upset or anything like that. I hope I'm explaining this right? I really do want to be helpful and answer your questions properly, Doctor.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Have you ever noticed that you need less sleep than usual, or that you feel just as energetic even if you haven’t slept as much as you normally would?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I certainly wouldn't say that. I mean, a good night's rest is quite important, isn't it? I wouldn't want you to think I'm staying up all hours of the night doing goodness-knows-what! \n\nIf I happen to miss my sleep for some reason, I definitely feel tired the next day, just like anyone else would. I might still try to keep my hands busy during the day, just to stick to my routine and be helpful, but I certainly don't feel full of energy if I haven't rested properly. I really do try my best to keep a very sensible bedtime schedule. I hope that's the right way to go about it? I'd hate for you to think I wasn't taking proper care of my health.\n\nDoctor: In situations where you’re expected to sit still, like watching a movie or waiting in a waiting room, do you find yourself needing to move or feel like you can’t stay seated even if there’s no task to do?\n\nPatient: Oh, my, no. I certainly wouldn't just get up and wander about in a waiting room or a movie theater! That would be terribly rude to the other people, wouldn't it? I was always taught to be very respectful of others in public spaces. \n\nIf I'm waiting to see my doctor—well, present company excepted, of course!—or watching a nice film, I just sit quietly. I mean, I might cross and uncross my ankles a bit, or perhaps thumb through one of those magazines they leave out, just to pass the time politely. But I certainly don't feel like I absolutely *can't* stay in my seat. I'd never want to make a fuss or draw attention to myself like that. I hope that makes sense? I always try to be on my very best behavior in those sorts of places, I promise.\n\nDoctor: When you're completely alone at home and there's absolutely nothing that needs to be done, do you still feel like you can't quite relax or settle down?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, if there's truly nothing to be done, I think I'm quite capable of just enjoying a quiet afternoon. I'm not a restless person, really, I promise. \n\nI might... well, I might find a little something to do, like straightening the doilies or maybe reorganizing my knitting basket, but that's just because I like keeping a tidy home, you know? It's not that I *can't* settle down. I just prefer to keep my hands busy rather than just staring at the walls. It feels nice to accomplish little things, even if it's just sorting out my buttons. That's perfectly normal, isn't it? I certainly wouldn't want you to think I'm pacing the halls or anything silly like that. I really do enjoy my peace and quiet.\n\nDoctor: Even when there’s no task to do and you're alone, do you ever feel an inner sense of restlessness or tension, like you can't quite settle your mind or body, even if you're not actively trying to be productive?\n\nPatient: Oh, an inner tension? Well... I suppose... maybe just a tiny bit, sometimes? I certainly don't want to sound like a bundle of nerves, because I'm really quite cheerful most of the time! \n\nI do sometimes get this... well, I call it an \"ants-in-my-chair\" feeling. It happens more when I'm sitting through the long announcements at church, or during the weekly meetings at the senior center. I just get a little fidgety, I suppose. I might shift around in my seat a bit, or perhaps excuse myself to get a quick cup of coffee, or just quietly straighten the papers on the table in front of me. Just little things to help me get through the sitting part, you know? \n\nIt's not that I'm terribly anxious or anything like that, I promise! I just feel a bit better if I can move just a smidge. That's not too unusual for someone my age, is it? I hope you don't think I'm being disruptive during church. I really try to be as quiet as a mouse about it.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: When you feel that inner restlessness or 'ants-in-my-chair' feeling, are you aware of needing to move just for the sake of moving, even when there’s no task to do or no goal in mind?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I wouldn't say it's just... moving for the sake of moving. That sounds a bit erratic, doesn't it? I certainly wouldn't want you to think I'm just wiggling about for no reason at all! \n\nWhenever I do shift around or get up, there's always a little purpose to it, I think. Even if it's just to... well, to smooth a wrinkle in my skirt or fetch a peppermint from my purse. It's never just moving with absolutely nothing in mind. I always try to be quite deliberate, you know? \n\nI hope I'm explaining that properly... I just wouldn't want to give the impression that I'm out of control of my own body or doing things without a reason. That would be dreadful. Does that make sense, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: When you feel that urge to move or fidget, are there times when you're not trying to accomplish anything specific, like adjusting your clothes or organizing something, but you just feel the need to move your hands or feet without a clear reason?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I really don't think so. I mean... just moving my hands or feet for absolutely no reason at all? That sounds a bit... well, a bit odd, doesn't it? I certainly wouldn't want you to think I'm just sitting there twitching or anything unseemly like that. \n\nIf I'm moving my foot, it's usually because it's fallen asleep, or maybe I'm just tapping along to a nice hymn in my head. And my hands... well, like I said, I like to keep them busy with my knitting or tidying. I don't think I ever just move them without knowing why. That would be quite strange, wouldn't it? I hope you don't think I'm doing things like that without realizing it. I'm usually very aware of what I'm doing. Is that the sort of answer you were looking for?\n\nDoctor: When you feel that need to shift around or move a bit, is it ever just a discomfort in your body that makes you want to move, even if there's nothing specific you're trying to fix or organize?\n\nPatient: Oh, just a general discomfort... well, I suppose... maybe? I mean, sometimes sitting in those wooden pews for a long time can make anyone's back a little stiff, don't you think? \n\nBut just a... a vague discomfort that makes me move for no reason? Oh, I don't think I'd call it that. That makes it sound like I'm restless in a bothersome way, and I'm really not! I'm quite content most of the time. If I shift, it's really just to get a little more comfortable, like anyone would. I'm sure you must adjust your posture in that chair of yours from time to time, right? \n\nI just... I want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture. I wouldn't want you to write down that I'm uncomfortable in my own skin or anything silly like that. I'm just a normal older lady trying to stay comfortable. That's perfectly alright, isn't it?\n\nDoctor: When you feel that urge to move or shift around, even slightly, is there ever a moment where you feel like it's not about being comfortable or doing a task, but more like your body just doesn't feel right unless you're doing something, even if it's small?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness. I... I really don't think my body ever feels like it *has* to be doing something just to feel right. That sounds so... so demanding, doesn't it? Like I'm at the mercy of my own nerves! \n\nI mean, I suppose sometimes I just prefer to be busy. It's nice to feel useful, even if it's just a small thing like folding a napkin or smoothing the tablecloth. But it's not because I feel wrong if I don't do it. It's just a preference, really. A habit of a lifetime, you could say. \n\nI certainly don't feel driven by some... some inner compulsion that I can't ignore. I wouldn't want you to think I'm sitting at home feeling all out of sorts unless my hands are moving! I'm quite peaceful, truly. I hope that's the kind of answer you need? I just want to be sure I'm giving you the right idea.\n\nDoctor: When you're completely still, like when you're lying in bed trying to fall asleep, do you ever feel like your body just wants to keep moving, even just a little, even if there's no reason to?\n\nPatient: Oh, lying in bed... well... no, not really. I mean, when it's time to sleep, I just close my eyes and try to rest. It would be quite awful to be lying there tossing and turning, feeling like I *had* to move, wouldn't it? I'm sure people who have that must be so exhausted. \n\nI suppose sometimes it takes a little while to settle in, but that's just because I'm thinking about the day or... or maybe my hip aches a tiny bit, which is perfectly normal at my age, you know. But I don't feel like my body is trying to keep moving on its own. I'm usually quite still once I'm tucked in. I wouldn't want you to think I'm thrashing about in the dark! I'm a very quiet sleeper, really. Does that sound normal to you? I hope I'm being helpful.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Do you ever notice any unusual sensations in your legs, like tingling, crawling, or an urge to move them, especially when you're sitting still or lying down?\n\nPatient: Oh, tingling or crawling? Oh my, no. That sounds terribly unpleasant, doesn't it? Like... like having bugs on you! No, I've never felt anything strange like that in my legs. \n\nI mean, sometimes my legs get a little heavy if I've been on my feet too long, perhaps doing a bit too much gardening or standing at the sink. But that's just a normal tiredness, I'm sure. It's certainly not an urge to move them when I'm trying to rest. When I sit down in my armchair with a cup of tea, my legs are very happy to just stay right where they are! \n\nI wouldn't want you to worry that I have some sort of... nervous condition in my legs. They're perfectly fine, just a bit older now. That's a good thing, isn't it? That I don't have those crawling feelings you mentioned? I do try to take good care of myself.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: When you're trying to rest or relax, especially at night in bed, do you ever feel a strong urge to move your legs, even if you're not aware of any discomfort or specific reason to move them?\n\nPatient: Oh, I feel like I must be giving you the exact same answer again, I'm so sorry! But no, truly, I don't feel any sort of... strong urge to move them. \n\nWhen it's time to relax, my legs are just as happy to rest as the rest of me. I just lie there quite peacefully. I hope it's alright that I keep saying no to these questions? I'd hate for you to think I'm being difficult or, oh, not paying proper attention to my own body. But they really are very well-behaved at night, I promise you. Am I doing alright with these answers? I just want to make sure I'm giving you what you need.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.     "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh... well... I suppose you've caught me there, Doctor. I... I do hope it doesn't make me sound foolish, but yes. That is actually... well, it's sort of what brings me in to see you today. 

For the past year or so, I just feel like I'm always... puttering about. Fixing little things around the house, arranging the cupboards, or volunteering for just one more thing. I mean, it's good to be helpful, isn't it? I always want to be useful to people. But... well, even at 87, I seem to have such a hard time just sitting down and being still. 

I know ladies my age are supposed to enjoy their rest, and I feel a bit embarrassed that I can't seem to just be at peace when there's nothing to do. I should be able to just sit quietly, shouldn't I? Is it terribly unusual for someone my age to be like this? I do try my best to relax, I really do... I hope that's the sort of answer you were looking for?

I'd hate for you to think I wasn't taking proper care of my health.\n\nDoctor: In situations where you’re expected to sit still, like watching a movie or waiting in a waiting room, do you find yourself needing to move or feel like you can’t stay seated even if there’s no task to do?\n\nPatient: Oh, my, no. I certainly wouldn't just get up and wander about in a waiting room or a movie theater! That would be terribly rude to the other people, wouldn't it? I was always taught to be very respectful of others in public spaces. \n\nIf I'm waiting to see my doctor—well, present company excepted, of course!—or watching a nice film, I just sit quietly. I mean, I might cross and uncross my ankles a bit, or perhaps thumb through one of those magazines they leave out, just to pass the time politely. But I certainly don't feel like I absolutely *can't* stay in my seat. I'd never want to make a fuss or draw attention to myself like that. I hope that makes sense? I always try to be on my very best behavior in those sorts of places, I promise.\n\nDoctor: When you're completely alone at home and there's absolutely nothing that needs to be done, do you still feel like you can't quite relax or settle down?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, if there's truly nothing to be done, I think I'm quite capable of just enjoying a quiet afternoon. I'm not a restless person, really, I promise. \n\nI might... well, I might find a little something to do, like straightening the doilies or maybe reorganizing my knitting basket, but that's just because I like keeping a tidy home, you know? It's not that I *can't* settle down. I just prefer to keep my hands busy rather than just staring at the walls. It feels nice to accomplish little things, even if it's just sorting out my buttons. That's perfectly normal, isn't it? I certainly wouldn't want you to think I'm pacing the halls or anything silly like that. I really do enjoy my peace and quiet.\n\nDoctor: Even when there’s no task to do and you're alone, do you ever feel an inner sense of restlessness or tension, like you can't quite settle your mind or body, even if you're not actively trying to be productive?\n\nPatient: Oh, an inner tension? Well... I suppose... maybe just a tiny bit, sometimes? I certainly don't want to sound like a bundle of nerves, because I'm really quite cheerful most of the time! \n\nI do sometimes get this... well, I call it an \"ants-in-my-chair\" feeling. It happens more when I'm sitting through the long announcements at church, or during the weekly meetings at the senior center. I just get a little fidgety, I suppose. I might shift around in my seat a bit, or perhaps excuse myself to get a quick cup of coffee, or just quietly straighten the papers on the table in front of me. Just little things to help me get through the sitting part, you know? \n\nIt's not that I'm terribly anxious or anything like that, I promise! I just feel a bit better if I can move just a smidge. That's not too unusual for someone my age, is it? I hope you don't think I'm being disruptive during church. I wouldn't say it's just... moving for the sake of moving. That sounds a bit erratic, doesn't it? I certainly wouldn't want you to think I'm just wiggling about for no reason at all! \n\nWhenever I do shift around or get up, there's always a little purpose to it, I think. Even if it's just to... well, to smooth a wrinkle in my skirt or fetch a peppermint from my purse. It's never just moving with absolutely nothing in mind. I always try to be quite deliberate, you know? \n\nI hope I'm explaining that properly... I just wouldn't want to give the impression that I'm out of control of my own body or doing things without a reason. That would be dreadful. Does that make sense, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: When you feel that urge to move or fidget, are there times when you're not trying to accomplish anything specific, like adjusting your clothes or organizing something, but you just feel the need to move your hands or feet without a clear reason?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I really don't think so. I mean... just moving my hands or feet for absolutely no reason at all? That sounds a bit... well, a bit odd, doesn't it? I certainly wouldn't want you to think I'm just sitting there twitching or anything unseemly like that. \n\nIf I'm moving my foot, it's usually because it's fallen asleep, or maybe I'm just tapping along to a nice hymn in my head.
